# Source Index - Claim-Level Hard-Number Ledger

**Status:** draft public claim ledger
**Created:** 2026-05-25
**Updated:** 2026-06-26
**Scope:** Rocket Lab Research data-center investigation source ledger

This file is the query surface for hard-number source status. It does not
replace the topic documents. It records which claims are externally certified,
which claims are derived from sourced inputs, which are scenario assumptions,
and which claims must not be presented as facts.

## Source Status Taxonomy

Use this exact public taxonomy in docs, code metadata, and JSON source fields.

| Source status | Meaning |
|---|---|
| `certified` | Directly supported by a primary or official source. |
| `sourced_estimate` | Estimated from credible external sources, but not an official exact value. |
| `derived_estimate` | Computed from sourced or scenario inputs using a stated formula. |
| `projection` | A forward-looking market or technical projection from an external source. |
| `extrapolation` | A project extrapolation from known or sourced behavior. |
| `scenario` | A chosen modeling assumption for feasibility analysis. |
| `placeholder` | A known open slot that should not support public claims. |
| `stale` | Known old material retained only for historical context. |

Default-model hard numbers may use `certified`, `sourced_estimate`,
`derived_estimate`, `projection`, `extrapolation`, or `scenario`. Public docs
must not cite `placeholder` or `stale` items as current evidence.

## How To Query This Wiki

Use this file when asking whether a number is safe to quote. Search by claim ID
or by topic prefix:

- `RLDC` - release-critical data-center default assumptions and public claims.
- `NTR` - Neutron payload, launch mode, cadence, and launch economics.
- `GPU` - AI hardware rack configuration, power, mass, and cost.
- `REV` - market size, revenue per watt, pricing, premiums, and venture cases.
- `THR` - thermal, solar, reliability, burn-in, and model-derived spacecraft mass.
- `COMM` - communications-track market sizes, carrier financials, spectrum and
  band economics, deployment costs, space-comms TAM, terrestrial laser, and the
  Falcon 9 cadence analogue (comms wave 1, ingested 2026-06-11), plus the wave-2
  dollar sizing of the rural/remote fringe, the premium/sovereign niche, and the
  consolidated new-entrant-addressable pool (ingested 2026-06-11), plus the wave-3
  supply-side cost stack, the ground incumbents' marginal-cost defend floor, and the
  ground-vs-space delivery-cost ratio (ingested 2026-06-17), plus the wave-4 framework
  inputs (spectrum generations and SCS access, the 4G-to-5G transition cost, the 6G
  no-premium demand read, the Starlink V3 capacity benchmark, the direct-to-cell market
  and physics, the Neutron comms-payload fit, the laser DC-interconnect side track, and
  the framework synthesis) (ingested 2026-06-18), plus the wave-5 coverage-and-
  spectrum inputs (the LEO continuous-coverage floor, the Starlink V3/V4 spectrum
  incorporation, the large-array fold ratio, and the spectrum-purchase economics)
  (ingested 2026-06-22), plus the wave-6 antenna-platform-and-DTC-architecture inputs
  (the Rocket Lab Flatellite platform, the Starlink V3 platform / why-Starship size
  story, the direct-to-cell antenna-aperture tradeoff, the single-governing DTC system
  model, the per-phone rate and latency operating point, and the DTC coverage geography)
  (ingested 2026-06-23), plus the wave-7 DTC supply-side inputs (the governing DTC system
  model landed, the per-satellite capacity supply and spectrum-saturation ceiling, the
  externally-sourced spectrum-capacity primer and its channels-add-up companion, the DTC
  spectrum-access routes, the data-rate-vs-spectrum sweep, the subscribers-per-satellite
  sizing, and the ground cellular cost-per-subscriber comparison) (ingested 2026-06-23 to
  2026-06-26). This is the master
  reconciliation of the ingest docs' colliding `COMM-###` namespaces plus the
  synthesis `COMM-S##` namespace into one global, non-colliding ledger. Wave 1
  occupies `COMM-001`..`COMM-056`; wave 2 continues at `COMM-057`..`COMM-079`; wave 3
  continues at `COMM-080`..`COMM-103`; wave 4 continues at `COMM-104`..`COMM-177`
  (with `COMM-140a` the V3 long-tail sub-row); wave 5 continues at `COMM-178`..`COMM-248`
  (with `COMM-191`..`COMM-196` left unused between the V3/V4-spectrum block ending at
  `COMM-190` and the folding block starting at `COMM-197`); wave 6 continues at
  `COMM-249`..`COMM-405`, each new doc minting its own in-doc ledger this time (no
  fold-into-long-tail), so the in-doc IDs and these global IDs are identical. This
  ledger is the single source of truth for the final `COMM-###` allocation, and the
  full wave-6 allocation is: Flatellite platform `COMM-249`..`COMM-270`; Starlink V3
  platform `COMM-271`..`COMM-292`; DTC antenna-aperture tradeoff `COMM-293`..`COMM-314`;
  the DTC system model `COMM-315`..`COMM-335` plus `COMM-356`..`COMM-365`; the per-phone
  rate and latency doc `COMM-336`..`COMM-355` plus `COMM-371`..`COMM-378`; and the DTC
  coverage-geography doc `COMM-386`..`COMM-405`. Two ranges are deliberately reserved and
  unused: `COMM-366`..`COMM-370` (the tail of the system-model integration block) and
  `COMM-379`..`COMM-385` (the tail of the per-phone aperture-curve block). The per-phone
  doc's IDs interleave with the system-model doc's across the `COMM-315`..`COMM-378` range
  but are non-overlapping by design (the per-phone doc owns 336-355 and 371-378; the model
  doc owns 315-335 and 356-365), so the interleave is not a collision. The
  `dtc_system_model.md` doc (its block `COMM-315`..`COMM-335` + `COMM-356`..`COMM-365`) is
  the GOVERNING MODEL and is recorded here as the source of truth for the architecture; it
  was reserved as a WIP placeholder during the wave-6 reconciliation and has since LANDED
  (committed in `research/`, expanded to its full per-claim ledger 2026-06-26). The
  wave-4 lint report's tentative earmark of `COMM-247`..`COMM-274` to the laser and
  framework docs was never materialized (those docs use local 1..N numbering and mint
  no `COMM-###`), so it is superseded by the actual wave-5/wave-6 allocation recorded
  here. Wave 7 (ingested 2026-06-23 to 2026-06-26, the DTC supply-side wave) continues at
  `COMM-406`..`COMM-560`: per-satellite capacity supply `COMM-406`..`COMM-425`; the
  spectrum-capacity primer `COMM-426`..`COMM-439` (`COMM-440`..`COMM-450` reserved-unused);
  the DTC spectrum-access doc `COMM-481`..`COMM-492` (`COMM-451`..`COMM-480` reserved-unused,
  declared by that doc but unminted); the rate-vs-spectrum doc `COMM-493`..`COMM-512`
  (`COMM-513`..`COMM-520` reserved-unused); the ground cost-per-subscriber doc
  `COMM-521`..`COMM-534`; and the subscribers-per-satellite doc `COMM-535`..`COMM-560`. The
  `channels_aggregate_answer.md` one-pager mints no IDs (it cross-refs the primer's
  `COMM-427`/`COMM-428`/`COMM-433`/`COMM-434`).

The topic documents remain the source of narrative reasoning. This ledger is the
fast pass/fail layer for source quality.

## Current Verdict

The research corpus is usable as research support when the docs preserve the
boundary between facts, estimates, derived model outputs, projections,
extrapolations, and scenarios. The highest-risk claims are Neutron SSO payload,
Neutron internal launch cost, future GPU rack mass/power/cost, orbital premium
revenue, five-year GPU service life, radiator/solar mass and cost, and ground
reference scope. Those are not deletion issues; they are wording and
source-status issues.

The ground reference is a derived five-year comparison assembled from
source-backed cost bands and explicit scenario choices. The promoted ground JSON
now carries the same per-input source statuses used below.

Reviewed conclusions and promoted model JSON do not belong in `research/`.
Research is the primary-source and reasoning layer. Static conclusions and
default model outputs belong under `data_center/`.

## NTR - Rocket Lab Neutron Claims

| ID | Claim or value | Source status | Role | Source trail | Uncertainty notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|

## COMM - Communications Claims (Wave 1)

This is the master reconciliation for communications wave 1 (ingested
2026-06-11). The ten ingest docs each restarted their own `COMM-###` numbering at
`COMM-001`, so those IDs collide across docs, and the synthesis used a separate
`COMM-S##` namespace. This section assigns one global, non-colliding `COMM-`
namespace (`COMM-001` .. `COMM-056`), grouping by doc in a stable order. The
ingest docs' own internal claim numbers are left unchanged; this ledger is the
master map. Each row's note records the source doc and that doc's internal claim
ID(s).

Source-status mapping from the docs' reading-guide tags: doc `[FACT]` maps to
`certified` when the source is a primary filing or official body (SEC, GSMA, ITU,
FCC, NASA, vendor datasheet) and to `sourced_estimate` when the "fact" is really a
market-research sizing; doc `[ESTIMATE]` maps to `sourced_estimate`, or to
`derived_estimate` when it is the doc's own arithmetic; doc `[PROJECTION]` maps to
`projection`. Every comms figure is communications-track research context, not a
data-center model input, unless a future cycle promotes it.

Coverage note: the load-bearing numbers (market sizes, carrier financials, the
willingness-to-pay curve, the C-band `$81B`, the cited-vs-served TAM and the 90%
haircut, the satellite 0.5% share, the spectrum-auction prices, the deployment
cost anchors, the optical scale numbers, and the Falcon 9 cadence spine) are
itemized. Each doc's remaining per-line items (individual carrier sub-metrics,
per-country auction decimals, vendor-by-vendor product rows) are folded into one
`long-tail` summary row per doc that cites the doc's internal claim-ID range
rather than repeating every line.

### COMM market and financial claims

| ID | Claim or value | Source status | Role | Source trail | Uncertainty notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `COMM-001` | US fixed broadband services market revenue is about `$70-95B/yr` (range `$63.6B` narrow to `$100.5B` North-America-wide). | `sourced_estimate` | Comms market sizing | [economics/comms_us_broadband_market.md](economics/comms_us_broadband_market.md); Grand View, Statista, Mordor | From `comms_us_broadband_market` (internal COMM-001..004). The `$70-95B` figure is a reasoned midpoint, not a filed number; research-firm definitions diverge ~40%. |
| `COMM-002` | US top-provider fixed broadband subscriber base is about `114.7M` (~76M cable, ~30.7M telco, ~13-14M FWA); ~115-130M total US connections. | `certified` | Comms market sizing | [economics/comms_us_broadband_market.md](economics/comms_us_broadband_market.md); Leichtman Research Group via Light Reading | From `comms_us_broadband_market` (internal COMM-007, COMM-008). 2023 base; FWA has grown materially since. |
| `COMM-003` | US broadband provider financial spine: Comcast 31.3M subs / ~$81B cap; Charter 29.7M / ~$20B; Verizon >16.3M / ~$191B; AT&T ~14.3M / ~$156B; T-Mobile ~8M FWA / ~$216B. | `certified` | Comms market context; carrier benchmark | [economics/comms_us_broadband_market.md](economics/comms_us_broadband_market.md); 2025-26 SEC filings, earnings, stockanalysis/companiesmarketcap | From `comms_us_broadband_market` (internal COMM-009..028). Whole-company financials (Comcast/AT&T/Verizon include non-broadband segments); market caps are June 17 2026 point-in-time. NOTE: Verizon cap is `~$191B` here but `~$201B` in `comms_us_cellular_market`/COMM-018, and AT&T cap `~$156B` here vs `~$160B` there (different aggregator timestamps); see the wave-1 lint report. |
| `COMM-004` | The largest US broadband subscriber bases carry the smallest market caps (Comcast/Charter de-rated vs the wireless balance sheets). | `sourced_estimate` | Comms structural read | [economics/comms_us_broadband_market.md](economics/comms_us_broadband_market.md) | From `comms_us_broadband_market` (the structural read). A neutrally-recorded observation, not a forecast; incumbent ARPU is a falling, not static, benchmark. |
| `COMM-005` | Broadband willingness-to-pay is sharply concave: about `$2.34/Mbps` at 4-10 Mbps collapsing to about `$0.02/Mbps` from 100 to 1,000 Mbps. | `sourced_estimate` | Load-bearing comms finding (WTP curve) | [economics/comms_us_broadband_market.md](economics/comms_us_broadband_market.md); Liu, Prince & Wallsten discrete-choice study (Information Economics and Policy 2018) | From `comms_us_broadband_market` (internal COMM-033, COMM-034). Peer-reviewed study; the marginal value of a megabit falls ~100x. The most robust finding in the comms base. |

| `COMM-049` | The same fog wall as the space link binds terrestrial laser: fog is 10-100 dB/km, a pure optical link reaches ~5 km enterprise-grade but only ~140 m for carrier-grade 99.999%; a hybrid FSO/RF link is the standard fix for five-nines. | `certified` | Terrestrial-laser weather constraint | [laser_comms/laser_terrestrial_interconnect.md](laser_comms/laser_terrestrial_interconnect.md); Wikipedia FSO, CableFree, McMaster/SPIE | From `laser_terrestrial_interconnect` (internal COMM-004..006). The ground-to-ground twin of the optical-ground-station site-diversity story; the laser needs RF backup just to match buried fiber on availability. |
| `COMM-050` | Terrestrial laser's incremental value: strong where fiber does not exist (deploy in hours vs trench a km, "dark fiber in the sky"); conditional where fiber exists (security, ~47%-faster latency, fast deploy), not cheaper or more reliable everyday. Beam spreads to only ~2 m at 1 km, hard to tap covertly. | `sourced_estimate` | Load-bearing incremental-value + security framing | [laser_comms/laser_terrestrial_interconnect.md](laser_comms/laser_terrestrial_interconnect.md); Light Reading (Taara), CableFree FSO guide | From `laser_terrestrial_interconnect` (internal COMM-007..009, plus the Section 3 synthesis). Security is physical-layer obscurity plus tamper-evidence, not crypto; the DC-interconnect use is nascent/single-vendor (Taara's own "early stages"); the FSO market-size dollars (internal COMM-010) are a wide analyst range. |
| `COMM-051` | Falcon 9 cadence took about 14 years to reach a 165/yr record (2025), bending up only ~2021-2022 once booster reuse was routine AND Starlink filled the manifest: 31 (2021), 61, 96, 134, 165. | `certified` | Load-bearing cadence spine | [competitors/falcon9_cadence_ramp.md](competitors/falcon9_cadence_ramp.md); Wikipedia launch list, ElonX, Data Explained | From `falcon9_cadence_ramp` (internal COMM-001..005). Counted public fact, cross-checked across trackers; minor year-to-year discrepancies (Falcon-9-only vs family) do not affect the shape. |
| `COMM-052` | Reuse maturation: first booster landing (Dec 2015) to first reuse (Mar 2017) was ~15 months; first reuse to reuse-driven high cadence (2022) was ~5 more years; Falcon 9 reuses the booster and fairing but NEVER the second stage. | `certified` | Load-bearing Starship-timeline basis | [competitors/falcon9_cadence_ramp.md](competitors/falcon9_cadence_ramp.md); Wikipedia reusable-launch-development, fairing-recovery program | From `falcon9_cadence_ramp` (internal COMM-006..010). Landing once is not flying often; Starship must rerun a harder curve (upper-stage reuse), so it does not inherit Falcon 9's cadence curve. |
| `COMM-053` | The Falcon 9 analogue supports "at least about 5 years" (not "about 3 years") to significant orbital data centers on Starship, with communications the credible near-term Starship payload; Starship flew 5 of a 25-flight 2025 target (a 5x miss). | `projection` | Load-bearing comms-vs-DC timeline read | [competitors/falcon9_cadence_ramp.md](competitors/falcon9_cadence_ramp.md); cross-ref [competitors/starship_addendum.md](competitors/starship_addendum.md) | From `falcon9_cadence_ramp` (internal COMM-018, plus Sections 3-4). A reasoned projection over an unproven vehicle, consistent with `starship_addendum`'s independently-derived 2028-2031 window and Starbase 25-launch cap; the downside risk is a fast SpaceX upper-stage-reuse solution. |
| `COMM-054` | Falcon-9 long-tail (all-time family launches and success rate, deep-reuse depth B1067/turnaround, fairing reflight counts, 2026 year-to-date and full-year guidance, derived year spans). | `certified` | Cadence detail (summary row) | [competitors/falcon9_cadence_ramp.md](competitors/falcon9_cadence_ramp.md) internal COMM-011..017 | Long-tail of `falcon9_cadence_ramp`; the ~9-day fastest-turnaround and the ~140-145 2026 guidance are single-source, flagged in-doc. |

headline claims mostly trace to the ingest docs already itemized above
(`COMM-S01`..`COMM-S27` map onto the market sizes, carrier financials, WTP curve,
C-band, satellite share, gaps, cited/served TAM, and optical scale numbers). The
two rows below capture the synthesis-level findings that are genuinely the
synthesis's own contribution rather than a re-statement of an ingest claim.

| ID | Claim or value | Source status | Role | Source trail | Uncertainty notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `COMM-055` | The founder's central comparison: data centers justify premium economics (demand outrunning supply, continual hardware upgrades, capacity expansion rewarded) precisely where communications does not (diminishing returns once users pass baseline broadband; capex a low-teens percent of revenue vs a multiple for AI DC). | `derived_estimate` | Load-bearing synthesis framing (stated as founder's working hypothesis) | [synthesis/comms_baseline_synthesis.md](synthesis/comms_baseline_synthesis.md) Section 4 (COMM-S27); [vision/comms_thesis.md](vision/comms_thesis.md) | Reconciles `comms_cellular_5g_deployment`/COMM-024 (capex intensity) with `comms_us_broadband_market`/COMM-005 (WTP curve). Recorded as the founder's working hypothesis to be tested in later waves, NOT a verdict; whether a space comms business escapes the trap by competing on reach/reliability/sovereignty/latency is the open thesis question. |
| `COMM-056` | The communications base offers no verdict on the Rocket Lab comms business; the served space-comms slice is the realistic ceiling (~$129B for a mature broad LEO play; a smaller higher-margin premium/sovereign niche for Rocket Lab, referenced from comms_business_case but unsized). | `derived_estimate` | Synthesis scope boundary | [synthesis/comms_baseline_synthesis.md](synthesis/comms_baseline_synthesis.md) (COMM-S20, COMM-S21); cross-ref [laser_comms/comms_business_case.md](laser_comms/comms_business_case.md) | The synthesis is isolated to communications and offers no business verdict; the premium/sovereign niche dollar size is the central open number (see RESEARCH_TRACKER backlog). The synthesis-namespace COMM-S## claims are reconciled into COMM-001..COMM-054 above. Wave 2 (below) closes this open number. |

## COMM - Communications Claims (Wave 2: dollar sizing)

This continues the master reconciliation for communications wave 2 (ingested
2026-06-11), the three dollar-sizing docs that close the two open numbers the
wave-1 thesis named (rural/remote fringe size; premium/sovereign niche size). The
two sizing docs EACH restarted their own `COMM-###` numbering at `COMM-057`, so
their internal IDs collide with each other, and the consolidation doc used its own
`COMM-082`..`COMM-090`. This section assigns one continuous global range,
`COMM-057`..`COMM-079`, with no gaps and no collision with the wave-1
`COMM-001`..`COMM-056`. The ingest docs' own internal claim numbers are left
unchanged; each row's note records the source doc and that doc's internal claim
ID(s), and where a consolidation-doc internal ID was remapped, the note states the
remap.

Source-status mapping from the docs' reading-guide tags follows the wave-1 rule:
doc `[FACT]` maps to `certified` when the source is a primary filing or official
body and to `sourced_estimate` when the "fact" is a market-research sizing; doc
`[ESTIMATE]` maps to `sourced_estimate`, or `derived_estimate` when it is the doc's
own arithmetic over sourced inputs; doc `[PROJECTION]` maps to `projection`; doc
`[ILLUSTRATIVE]` (an order-of-magnitude analytical framing, not an external
forecast) maps to `scenario`. Every wave-2 figure is demand-side communications
research context, not a data-center model input, unless a future cycle promotes it.
The load-bearing numbers (the rural-fringe ranges, the premium/sovereign gross pool
and open slice, the consolidated band, and the shares versus the $1.6T and $129B
anchors) are itemized; each doc's remaining per-line items (the per-tier ARPU rows,
the segment sub-totals, the per-program figures) are folded into one `long-tail`
summary row per doc citing that doc's internal claim-ID range.

The exact dollar attribution is reasoned, not modeled. |

The honest center of gravity is the conservative-to-mid band; the top end requires several favorable trends at once (price-driven emerging-market expansion that compresses ARPU, a meaningful direct-to-cell add-on, and allied governments buying managed service rather than building). |

## COMM - Communications Claims (Wave 3: supply-side cost and the ground-vs-space ratio)

This continues the master reconciliation for communications wave 3 (ingested
2026-06-17), the three cost-side docs that build the space supply cost, the ground
incumbents' marginal-cost defend floor, and the headline ground-vs-space delivery-
cost ratio. The three docs use INTERNAL `COMM-###` numbering that collides and
gaps: the space supply-cost doc ran internal `COMM-080`..`COMM-108`, the incumbent-
margins doc ran its own `COMM-080`..`COMM-103` (with gaps), and the cost-ratio doc
ran internal `COMM-109`..`COMM-117`. This section assigns one continuous global
range, `COMM-080`..`COMM-103`, continuing contiguously from the wave-2 tail
(`COMM-079`) with no gaps and no collision with `COMM-001`..`COMM-079`. The ingest
docs' own internal claim numbers are left unchanged; each row's note records the
source doc and that doc's internal claim-ID(s), and states the remap. Source-status
mapping follows the wave-1/2 rule (doc `[FACT]` to `certified` for a primary filing
or official body and to `sourced_estimate` for a market-research/trade-press sizing;
doc `[ESTIMATE]` to `sourced_estimate` or `derived_estimate` for the doc's own
arithmetic; doc `[DERIVED]` to `derived_estimate`; doc `[PROJECTION]` to
`projection`; doc `[ILLUSTRATIVE]` to `scenario`). Every wave-3 figure is supply-
side communications research context, not a data-center model input, unless a future
cycle promotes it. The load-bearing numbers (the space ~$480-680/sub/yr all-in, the
incumbent marginal floor ~$84-180/sub/yr, both flavor ratios, the Starlink-actual
segment margins, the BEAD corroboration) are itemized; each doc's remaining per-line
items are folded into one `long-tail` summary row per doc citing that doc's internal
claim-ID range.

From [economics/comms_space_supply_cost.md](economics/comms_space_supply_cost.md);

Per-sat cost rises across generations while per-bit cost falls sharply. | `sourced_estimate` | Space cost: satellite hardware line | [economics/comms_space_supply_cost.md](economics/comms_space_supply_cost.md) Section 1.1; NBF/Quilty, Planet Tech News, Basenor | Internal claims COMM-080, COMM-081, COMM-082. All third-party estimates (SpaceX discloses none); the V2 mini carries a ~2x source disagreement (Quilty ~$800k vs alt ~$250k), flagged. The trend (rising per-sat, falling per-bit) is robust regardless of the point estimate. |
| `COMM-081` | Starlink V3 per-satellite downlink capacity ~`1 Tbps` (~4 Tbps incl. laser backhaul); ~`60 Tbps` added per Starship launch (>20x a Falcon 9 V2-mini launch). | `projection` | Space cost: capacity-per-generation | [economics/comms_space_supply_cost.md](economics/comms_space_supply_cost.md) Sections 1.1, 2.1; Tom's Hardware, Via Satellite | Internal claim COMM-083. Carried from the laser-comms base; the per-OGS figure is FACT-anchored (Cailabs/DataPath), the hub and full-network figures are flagged ESTIMATE in the source doc. |
| `COMM-087` | Starlink RF ground segment: ~`150` operational gateway stations (~170 incl. construction, early 2026); user-terminal production cost ~`$2,400` (2020) to ~`$500-600` (2023), sold ~$349 (subsidized below cost early). | `sourced_estimate` | Space cost: RF ground + terminal subsidy | [economics/comms_space_supply_cost.md](economics/comms_space_supply_cost.md) Section 3.1; Starlink Insider, Basenor | Internal claims COMM-095, COMM-096. The terminal subsidy was a major early margin drag the S-1 readers cite as one of three margin tailwinds; a B2B service with professional customer-funded terminals partly avoids it. |

The constellation is a treadmill rebuilt every ~5 years, not a built asset that then runs cheaply. | `sourced_estimate` | Space cost: the defining recurring line (SINGLE-LINEAGE FLAG) | [economics/comms_space_supply_cost.md](economics/comms_space_supply_cost.md) Section 4.1; Motley Fool, SpaceXChart | Internal claims COMM-097, COMM-098. The ~`$6-8B/yr` replacement capex and ~1,000 sats/yr rate are a SINGLE 2024 analyst lineage (Motley Fool) and are arithmetically low against a ~10,000-sat fleet on a 5-yr life (which implies ~2,000+/yr); flagged. This single-lineage figure is the load-bearing input to the ~$200-260/sub/yr space-specific split (COMM-091) and is the wave-3 lint report's top FACT-tag caution. |
| `COMM-089` | SpaceX whole-company 2025 (S-1): revenue `$18.7B` ($11.4B Starlink, $4B Space, $3.2B AI); `-$4.9B` net loss; `-$2.6B` operating loss; `$6.6B` adjusted EBITDA. Audited S-1 filing, cross-checked across readers; the company-vs-segment distinction is the main interpretation risk and is handled explicitly. The delivery economics live in the segment (next row), not the whole company. |
| `COMM-090` | Starlink connectivity SEGMENT 2025 (S-1): operating income `$4.42B` on `$11.4B` revenue (~`38.6%` operating margin); adjusted EBITDA `$7.17B` (~`63%` margin); subscribers 8.9M (end 2025) / 10.3M (Q1 2026); ARPU `$99/mo` (2023) to `$81` (2025) to `$66` (Q1 2026). | `certified` | Space cost: the disclosed segment anchor (load-bearing) | [economics/comms_space_supply_cost.md](economics/comms_space_supply_cost.md) Sections 5.0-5.1; SpaceXChart, New Space Economy, Via Satellite S-1 | Internal claims COMM-101, COMM-102, COMM-103, COMM-104. The segment numbers are the correct denominator for "what does it cost to deliver communications from space"; cross-checked across three readers. ARPU is FALLING (mix shift), so margin is held by cost falling faster. This is the anchor for COMM-091 and for the cost-ratio doc's realistic-for-SpaceX finding (COMM-103). |
| `COMM-091` | Implied all-in space delivery cost (mature incumbent): ~`$480-680/subscriber/yr` all-in (revenue minus operating income), of which the space-specific satellite+launch replacement portion is ~`$200-260/sub/yr`; network-average ~`$0.05-0.30/GB` (vs ~$0.50/GB metered retail) but RISING sharply with user density; capital per Gbps ~$10k (V2 mini) to ~$1.2k (V3). | `derived_estimate` | Space cost: the load-bearing per-subscriber/per-GB headline | [economics/comms_space_supply_cost.md](economics/comms_space_supply_cost.md) Sections 5.1-5.3; from S-1 + replacement capex | Internal claims COMM-105, COMM-106, COMM-107. The ~$480-680/sub/yr all-in is anchored to disclosed segment revenue and operating income (solid); the ~$200-260 space-specific split LEANS ON the single-lineage ~$6-8B/yr replacement capex (COMM-088), so it inherits that softness. The per-GB figure is the softest number (utilization-dependent). This is the SPACE numerator the cost-ratio doc consumes. |
| `COMM-092` | Space supply-cost long-tail: the per-beam density constraint (oversubscription ~30 beam-service-locations/sq-mi, "~700 Gbps serves several thousand"), and the conservative new-entrant case (small scale, no amortization denominator) where per-subscriber delivered cost is `multiples higher` than the incumbent because the fixed stack spreads over far fewer subscribers. Scale is the whole game. | `sourced_estimate` | Space cost: detail (density ceiling + new-entrant case) | [economics/comms_space_supply_cost.md](economics/comms_space_supply_cost.md) Sections 5.2, 5.4; XLab, Jeff Geerling | Internal claims COMM-108 and Section 5.4. The density constraint (cost rises with user density, the opposite of fiber) is the permanent structural ceiling. The new-entrant case is a STRUCTURAL argument (fixed-cost-dominated, denominator-driven), not a sourced model; direction high-confidence, magnitudes illustrative. |

### COMM ground incumbent margins and the marginal-cost defend floor (wave 3)

From [economics/comms_incumbent_margins_competitive_floor.md](economics/comms_incumbent_margins_competitive_floor.md);
internal namespace COMM-080..103 (with gaps; collides with the supply-cost doc),
remapped to global COMM-093..099.

| ID | Claim or value | Source status | Role | Source trail | Uncertainty notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `COMM-093` | US carrier/cable EBITDA margins cluster at ~`36-41%` (FY2025, consolidated or relevant segment): Comcast Connectivity & Platforms ~37-41%, Charter ~41.4%, Verizon ~36%, AT&T ~38.6%, T-Mobile ~38% of total revenue (~47.5% of service revenue). This is the headroom a defending incumbent can sacrifice in a price war and stay cash-positive. | `certified` | Incumbent margin headroom (load-bearing) | [economics/comms_incumbent_margins_competitive_floor.md](economics/comms_incumbent_margins_competitive_floor.md) Section 1.1; Comcast/Charter/Verizon/AT&T/T-Mobile FY2025 releases, MacroTrends, Morningstar | Internal claims COMM-080, COMM-081, COMM-091, COMM-092, COMM-093 (margins doc). Each computed from the company's own FY2025 release and cross-checked; the ~36-41% clustering is firm. Comcast is on a segment basis and T-Mobile's ~47.5% is on service revenue, both stated so they are not misread. |
| `COMM-094` | Cable broadband gross/cash margin runs ~`80-90%` (single operator up to ~97%) because the plant is sunk and the marginal cost of carrying one more existing household's traffic is `< 1 cent/GB` on a fixed network (transit a fraction of a penny). | `sourced_estimate` | The structural reason the defend floor is low (load-bearing) | [economics/comms_incumbent_margins_competitive_floor.md](economics/comms_incumbent_margins_competitive_floor.md) Sections 1.2, 2.1; Cablefax, Next TV, Stop the Cap/WSJ, HuffPost, BroadbandNow, ICLE | Internal claims COMM-094, COMM-095 (margins doc). The ~80-90% gross margin is TRIANGULATED across four outlets but operator-/journalist-quoted, not one audited analyst decomposition, and several press sources predate 2015; flagged in the wave-3 lint report. Treat ~90% as "high, order-of-magnitude correct," not a precise constant. The <1 cent/GB fixed marginal cost is FACT. |
| `COMM-095` | Mobile marginal cost to deliver a GB to a device is ~`$0.50-$1.50/GB` (vs ~$4.64/GB average US retail, 2018), higher than fixed because radio/RAN capacity is genuinely shared and congestible. | `sourced_estimate` | The mobile defend floor (SINGLE-SOURCE FLAG) | [economics/comms_incumbent_margins_competitive_floor.md](economics/comms_incumbent_margins_competitive_floor.md) Section 2.2; LinkedIn/Tom Allen, Statista | Internal claims COMM-096, COMM-097 (margins doc). The ~`$0.50-$1.50/GB` delivery cost is SINGLE-SOURCE (one industry commentator) and is flagged; it is cross-checked only against the carriers' willingness to wholesale spare capacity to the cable MVNOs at that incremental logic. The wave-3 lint report carries this as a FACT-tag caution. |
| `COMM-096` | The fixed-broadband defend floor: the marginal/cash cost to serve one more already-passed broadband sub is ~`10-20% of ARPU` (~`$7-15/sub/month` against a ~$73.65 cable ARPU). A cable operator could halve a defended customer's price and still clear its cash cost. | `derived_estimate` | The price-to-beat in served fixed markets (load-bearing) | [economics/comms_incumbent_margins_competitive_floor.md](economics/comms_incumbent_margins_competitive_floor.md) Sections 2.1, 2.3 | Internal claim COMM-098 (margins doc). The clean inverse of the ~80-90% gross margin (COMM-094) applied to the filed cable ARPU; the firmer of the two floors. This ~$84-180/sub/yr is the GROUND-MARGINAL denominator the cost-ratio doc's flavor (b) consumes. |
| `COMM-097` | Incumbent headroom to absorb a defensive price cut: ~`30-40 percentage points of EBITDA` and ~`70-80 points of broadband gross margin`. The incumbent can cut toward the marginal floor and stay operating-cash-flow positive, soaking the cut out of margin rather than going cash-negative. | `derived_estimate` | The "lose margin vs lose money" gap | [economics/comms_incumbent_margins_competitive_floor.md](economics/comms_incumbent_margins_competitive_floor.md) Sections 1, 3.1 | Internal claim COMM-099 (margins doc). Derived from the sourced EBITDA and gross margins; the basis for why a space entrant that merely beats the incumbent's list/all-in price has won nothing (it must beat the marginal floor). Non-price defenses (latency, bundle lock-in, switching costs) stack on top. |
| `COMM-098` | The served-market price a space entrant must beat, by territory: fixed broadband, the incumbent's marginal ~10-20% of ARPU (~$7-15/mo), NOT the ~$74 list; mobile, the incumbent's incremental ~$0.50-1.50/GB plus a thin per-line cost, well under the ~$50-57 ARPU; unserved fringe, NO incumbent floor (it would pay the full $3,000-$200,000+ per passing to enter). | `derived_estimate` | The conclusion: price-to-beat by territory (load-bearing) | [economics/comms_incumbent_margins_competitive_floor.md](economics/comms_incumbent_margins_competitive_floor.md) Sections 2-5 | Internal claims COMM-100, COMM-101, COMM-102 (margins doc). Interpretation grounded in the sourced margins, per-GB costs, and ARPUs. The unserved-fringe no-floor is exactly why the wave-2 addressable dollars concentrate in the fringe; the two findings lock together. |
| `COMM-099` | The comms-vs-data-center asymmetry: in a SERVED market the ground competitor is not a fresh build but an entrenched incumbent pricing to its marginal cost, whereas the data-center track's 1.92x ground side is a greenfield build paying full freight. A space entrant cheaper than the incumbent's all-in cost is NOT necessarily cheaper than its defend cost. | `derived_estimate` | The conceptual core (interpretation) | [economics/comms_incumbent_margins_competitive_floor.md](economics/comms_incumbent_margins_competitive_floor.md) Section 4 | Internal claim COMM-103 (margins doc). Structural interpretation; the 1.92x data-center ratio is carried from the data-center track, not re-derived. The incumbent's sunk investment, irrelevant in the data-center price war, becomes a near-impregnable moat in the comms served-market price war. The fringe is where this asymmetry disappears. |

### COMM ground-vs-space cost ratio consolidation (wave 3)

From [economics/comms_ground_vs_space_cost_ratio.md](economics/comms_ground_vs_space_cost_ratio.md);
internal namespace COMM-109..117, remapped to global COMM-100..103. This is the
wave-3 consolidation doc: it runs no new cost build, it puts the space numerator
(COMM-091) and the ground stacks (COMM-096 marginal; the wave-1 deployment docs for
the fresh build) on a common per-subscriber-per-year basis to produce the headline
ratio.

| ID | Claim or value | Source status | Role | Source trail | Uncertainty notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `COMM-100` | Flavor (a), space vs a FRESH GROUND BUILD (the unserved comparison, the true mirror of the data-center 1.92x): annualized fresh rural-fiber build is ~`$875/yr` (low) to ~`$1,540/yr` (high) per subscriber (~$490 suburban, ~$44,500 extreme tail), so ground/space is ~`1.3-3.2x` rural (space BELOW ground), rising to ~`65-90x` in the extreme tail. SPACE IS CHEAPER in unserved territory. | `derived_estimate` | The headline ratio, flavor (a) (load-bearing) | [economics/comms_ground_vs_space_cost_ratio.md](economics/comms_ground_vs_space_cost_ratio.md) Section 2; from comms_broadband_deployment_economics.md (passing/connect/take-rate) + COMM-091 space cost | Internal claims COMM-109, COMM-110. Arithmetic on sourced ranges. The fresh-build side is annualized over a ~25-yr fiber life at a ~9% capital charge (COMM-102); reasonable alternative assumptions (shorter life, higher WACC) would RAISE the ground number and WIDEN space's advantage, so the ratio is conservative against space. Runs the OPPOSITE direction to the data-center 1.92x. |
| `COMM-101` | Flavor (b), space vs the INCUMBENT'S MARGINAL COST (the served comparison the data-center track never faces): space ~$480-680/sub/yr vs ground-marginal ~`$84-180/sub/yr` gives space/ground-marginal ~`3-8x` (space ABOVE the floor); per-GB ~5-30x+ above fixed. SPACE IS COSTLIER in served territory. | `derived_estimate` | The headline ratio, flavor (b) (load-bearing) | [economics/comms_ground_vs_space_cost_ratio.md](economics/comms_ground_vs_space_cost_ratio.md) Section 3; from COMM-096 (incumbent marginal) + COMM-091 (space) | Internal claim COMM-111. A clean read of the sourced marginal-cost floor against the sourced space cost; uses the MATURE incumbent space cost, so a small new space entrant (multiples higher per subscriber, COMM-092) sits far further above the floor. The two opposed ratios are the entire story: the single ratio the founder asked for genuinely does not exist for comms. |
| `COMM-102` | The annualization basis for flavor (a): fiber asset life ~`25 years` (IRS class life 24 yr; industry 20-25 yr) at a ~`9%` capital charge (the low end of the sourced ~10-15% fiber IRR hurdle). Corroborated by real 2025-26 BEAD procurement choosing satellite over fiber in the high-cost tail (fiber ~$100k/location rejected; Maine ~9,000 locations on Starlink; CO satellite ~$363M vs fiber ~$464M requests). | `sourced_estimate` | Flavor-(a) assumptions + real-world corroboration | [economics/comms_ground_vs_space_cost_ratio.md](economics/comms_ground_vs_space_cost_ratio.md) Sections 1, 2.3; POTs and PANs/TFI, Beyond Telecom Law Blog, Colorado Sun, Broadband Breakfast | Internal claims COMM-112, COMM-113. The fiber asset life is FACT (IRS/industry); the ~9% capital charge is an ASSUMPTION stated so it can be re-run. The BEAD procurement evidence is FACT (2+ sources) and is the independent cross-check that the flavor-(a) arithmetic is happening in real procurement now. |
| `COMM-103` | The required cost level and the asymmetry verdict: a space operator needs ~`$480-680/sub/yr` all-in (space-specific ~$200-260) to earn the ~$45-150B addressable pool at a ~38% op / ~63% EBITDA margin, and that level is NOT aspirational, it is Starlink's DISCLOSED 2025 actual (already achieved at SpaceX scale, but denominator-driven and unreachable for a small constellation). The verdict is an ASYMMETRY: space wins on cost in the unserved/remote fringe and premium/sovereign layer (no incumbent floor), loses in dense served markets (incumbent defends ~3-8x below space cost); the ratio flips on whether ground plant already exists, and space wins exactly where the wave-2 demand sits. | `derived_estimate` | The wave-3 verdict (load-bearing: realistic-for-SpaceX + the asymmetry + crossover) | [economics/comms_ground_vs_space_cost_ratio.md](economics/comms_ground_vs_space_cost_ratio.md) Sections 5-6; from COMM-090 (Starlink actual), COMM-091, COMM-100, COMM-101 | Internal claims COMM-114, COMM-115, COMM-116, COMM-117. The realistic-for-SpaceX half is HIGH-confidence on the fact (it is the disclosed S-1 actual, COMM-090) and medium on the scale caveat (the supply-cost doc's structural new-entrant argument, COMM-092). The asymmetry/crossover is an ESTIMATE/interpretation. Crossover sits ~dense-suburban fringe (~$490/sub/yr fresh build ~= ~$480-680 space), a zone not a point. The two independent lines (revenue at COMM-075, cost here) landing on the same map is the load-bearing convergence. No business verdict; the entrant-specific (non-Starlink) cost stack remains unmodeled. |

## COMM - Communications Claims (Wave 4: spectrum generations, the forced-6G cycle, the V3/D2C benchmark, the Neutron fit, and the framework)

This continues the master reconciliation for communications wave 4 (ingested
2026-06-18), the framework wave: seven source docs (spectrum generations and SCS
access, the 4G-to-5G transition cost, the 6G no-premium demand read, the Starlink
V3 capacity benchmark, the direct-to-cell market and physics, the Neutron
comms-payload fit, and the laser DC-interconnect side track) plus the framework
synthesis. Each source doc ran its OWN internal numbering (some restarting at 1,
some unnumbered, the framework citing source-doc internal numbers); this section
assigns one continuous global range, `COMM-104`..`COMM-177`, continuing
contiguously from the wave-3 tail (`COMM-103`) with no gaps and no collision with
`COMM-001`..`COMM-103`. NOTE on numbering: the `COMM-104`..`COMM-117` strings that
appear inside the wave-1/2/3 ledger NOTES are internal-claim references, not global
rows; the true highest global ROW id before this wave is `COMM-103`, so wave 4
correctly begins at `COMM-104`. Source-status mapping follows the wave-1/2/3 rule
(doc `[FACT]` to `certified` for a primary filing or official body (FCC, ITU, 3GPP,
SEC, GSMA, a vendor datasheet) and to `sourced_estimate` for a market-research or
trade-press sizing; doc `[FACT, single-source]` to `sourced_estimate` with a
single-source flag; doc `[ESTIMATE]` to `sourced_estimate` or `derived_estimate`;
doc `[DERIVED]` to `derived_estimate`; doc `[PROJECTION]` to `projection`). Every
wave-4 figure is communications-track research context, not a data-center model
input, unless a future cycle promotes it. RECONCILED, NOT DUPLICATED: where a
wave-4 claim restates a number already held in waves 1 to 3 (the ~$480-680/sub/yr
space cost at COMM-091/COMM-103, the two-flavor ratio at COMM-100/COMM-101, the
~$84-180/sub/yr incumbent floor at COMM-096, the ~$45-150B addressable pool at
COMM-075, the ~$129B served slice at COMM-037, the $1.6T/$1.1T cited TAM at
COMM-035/COMM-036, the ~450 Tbps / fleet anchors at COMM-082, the V3 ~1 Tbps /
~60-per-Starship / per-Gbps cost-down at COMM-081, the C-band ~$81B at
COMM-026/COMM-040, the ~$20-50K 5G site upgrade at COMM-025, the no-5G-ARPU-premium
finding at COMM-028, the 1.5x revenue multiple at RLDC-REVENUE-MULTIPLE-1_5X /
REV-008, and the Taara/fog-wall terrestrial-laser facts at COMM-048/COMM-049/
COMM-050), the existing global id is cited rather than a new one minted. Each doc's
remaining per-line items are folded into one `long-tail` summary row per doc citing
that doc's internal claim range.

### COMM spectrum generations and SCS access (wave 4)

| ID | Claim or value | Source status | Role | Source trail | Uncertainty notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `COMM-104` | A cellular "generation" (G) is a STANDARD and capability set, not a frequency (G = generation index): 3G = IMT-2000, 4G = IMT-Advanced, 5G = IMT-2020 (eMBB/URLLC/mMTC); 5G NR spans ~67 FR1 bands (410 MHz to 7.125 GHz) and ~11 FR2 bands (24.25 to 71 GHz), i.e. 600 MHz to 39 GHz, not a single "5 GHz" band; cellular 5G and 5 GHz Wi-Fi are unrelated. | `certified` | The "G is a standard not a band" correction (load-bearing) | [direct_communication/spectrum_generations_and_availability.md](direct_communication/spectrum_generations_and_availability.md) Sections 1-2; Data Alliance, Commsbrief, Wikipedia 5G NR bands, RF Page | Internal claims 1-5. Multi-source on each definition; corroborates the existing `spectrum_fundamentals_economics` framing (COMM-041). |
| `COMM-105` | A radio standard is portable across a band (refarming, and Dynamic Spectrum Sharing letting 4G LTE and 5G NR share one channel at ~1 ms granularity), but a band remains an exclusive single-occupant license; hard refarming is a slow multi-year drain-and-relight, and US carriers have largely SUNSET DSS (AT&T shuttered most DSS in 2021) for dedicated 5G channels. | `certified` | Spectrum portability vs exclusivity (load-bearing for the buy-vs-partner question) | [direct_communication/spectrum_generations_and_availability.md](direct_communication/spectrum_generations_and_availability.md) Section 2.2; Celona, Devopedia, Samsung white paper, Light Reading, Fierce | Internal claims 6, 7, 9. Quote the union, not either subset, to avoid a false contradiction. |

This is why direct-to-cell is a coverage/fill-in layer, not a capacity layer. |

| `COMM-167` | FSO is two-to-five orders of magnitude short of the primary AI-DCI job: matching one 121.6 Tbps fiber pair needs ~4,860 Taara Beams; matching 5 Pbit/s needs ~`200,000` beams; high-rate FSO (2 Tbps field experiment; ~1 Pbit/s lab-only) exists only on satellite/HAPS-grade terminals or in the lab. | `derived_estimate` | The bandwidth DISQUALIFICATION (load-bearing) | [laser_comms/laser_dc_interconnect_viability.md](laser_comms/laser_dc_interconnect_viability.md) Section 2; DERIVED (claims 4, 5, 7 arithmetic), NICT, arXiv 2305.12208, Nature Communications | Internal claims 8, 9 (DERIVED). The disqualification is arithmetic and robust regardless of the single-source petabit inputs; terrestrial laser cannot be the petabit AI-DCI backbone. |
| `COMM-168` | The distances, however, ARE in laser's wheelhouse: fiber adds ~4.9 us/km (~0.098 ms at 20 km to ~0.490 ms at 100 km), so ~1 ms-tolerant synchronous training is confined to a ~100 km radius; AI DC distance tiers are campus ~2 km (to ~20 km across 5-6 buildings), metro up to ~120 km, long-haul 500-1,000+ km, with Google's paired campuses ~15-50 miles apart. | `sourced_estimate` | The distance regime (laser-suited) | [laser_comms/laser_dc_interconnect_viability.md](laser_comms/laser_dc_interconnect_viability.md) Section 3; Lynx Planning, Corning, SDxCentral, Marvell; Google campus distances single-source (SemiAnalysis) | Internal claims 10, 11. The distances are squarely in laser's range; the bandwidth (COMM-167), not distance, is what disqualifies FSO for the headline job. |
| `COMM-169` | The real terrestrial laser DC niche is a TENS-OF-GBPS SUPPLEMENT (gap-fill, route-diversity/redundancy, obstacle-hop, secure fast-deploy), not the petabit backbone, which belongs to trenched coherent fiber: trenched DCI fiber takes 12-24 months to permit/build in dense areas, costs ~`$60k-120k/mile` (45% civil works), and uses 864-1,728 strands on DC routes. NVIDIA Spectrum-XGS targets cross-DC AI fabrics over hundreds of km (~1.9x communication improvement). | `sourced_estimate` | The terrestrial niche (narrow supplement) | [laser_comms/laser_dc_interconnect_viability.md](laser_comms/laser_dc_interconnect_viability.md) Sections 4-5; Global Data Center Hub, Data Center Knowledge, NVIDIA, SDxCentral | Internal claims 12, 13. The NVIDIA Spectrum-XGS ~1.9x is single-source. The terrestrial laser case is a narrow supplement, NOT a comms-revenue opportunity the project should chase on bandwidth. |
| `COMM-170` | Orbital data-center-to-data-center laser links are the PRIMARY, winning architecture in the one arena where fiber is not an option: operational/announced today (Axiom + Kepler at 2.5 Gbps, a SpaceX filing at 1 Tbps, Google Project Suncatcher laser-linked TPU clusters); laser ISL is the only credible orbital interconnect medium, and the natural home for Rocket Lab's Mynaric optical-terminal asset. | `certified` | The orbital case (strategically relevant) | [laser_comms/laser_dc_interconnect_viability.md](laser_comms/laser_dc_interconnect_viability.md) Section 6; Data Center Frontier, comms_business_case.md, optical_comms.md | Internal claim 15. The strategically relevant conclusion: in orbit, direct laser DC-to-DC is the primary architecture (no fiber in space), distinct from the RF consumer spine and using a per-link, not per-subscriber, unit. |
| `COMM-171` | Laser DC-interconnect long-tail: the ~504x AI-scale-up-traffic figure and tri-versity/quad-versity route-diversity demand (single-source, Big Fiber CCO via Data Center Knowledge), and the millimeter-wave (60 GHz to ~40 Gbps full-duplex) / microwave (10-11 GHz backup) wireless-DCI research and patent direction (also multi-Gbps and weather-limited; intra-DC FSO similarly research-stage). | `sourced_estimate` | Laser DC-interconnect detail (summary row) | [laser_comms/laser_dc_interconnect_viability.md](laser_comms/laser_dc_interconnect_viability.md) internal claims 3, 14 | Long-tail; the ~504x figure is single-source (flagged), and the mmWave/microwave-DCI direction is established research/patent but research-stage. (This doc's 15 internal items map to COMM-164..171, eight global rows; the side-track's lower load-bearing count justifies the tighter fold.) |

### COMM framework synthesis (wave 4)

From [synthesis/comms_framework_synthesis.md](synthesis/comms_framework_synthesis.md);
internal claims-ledger items 1..13. The framework is largely a RECONCILIATION of
wave-1-to-4 numbers into a model shape; its restated claims point at the source-doc
ids above and are NOT re-minted. The genuinely NEW framework-level claims map to
global COMM-172..177.

| ID | Claim or value | Source status | Role | Source trail | Uncertainty notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `COMM-172` | The comms model UNIT is cost per subscriber per year and per GB, DENSITY-AWARE: space cost-per-subscriber RISES with user density (Shannon-times-footprint, no densification, COMM-108), the inverse of terrestrial. This bends every part of the chain. | `derived_estimate` | The density-aware unit (NEW framework contribution, load-bearing) | [synthesis/comms_framework_synthesis.md](synthesis/comms_framework_synthesis.md) Section 1; DERIVED from COMM-108 (beam geometry), COMM-109 (area-capacity gap), COMM-145/COMM-147 (per-beam, per-GB) | Internal claim 1. The framework-level synthesis of the wave-4 capacity-physics inputs into the model's unit; the structural twist that distinguishes the comms model from the data-center model. |
| `COMM-173` | The model CHAIN is: space cost per subscriber x `1.5` revenue multiple (for ~30% regular margin) compared FORWARD against ground's NEXT-upgrade cost (not paid-off plant); the forward comparison FORKS on whether ground plant already exists (the two-flavor fork). The 1.5x is conservative against Starlink's disclosed actual (38.6% operating margin implies revenue at ~1.6x all-in delivery cost). | `derived_estimate` | The model chain and forward fork (NEW framework contribution, load-bearing) | [synthesis/comms_framework_synthesis.md](synthesis/comms_framework_synthesis.md) Sections 2.2-2.3; data_center/conclusion.md (the 1.5x/forward method), COMM-100/COMM-101 (the two-flavor fork), COMM-090 (38.6% margin) | Internal claims 3, 4. The 1.5x multiple is the existing project rule (RLDC-REVENUE-MULTIPLE-1_5X, REV-008), here applied to the comms cost-per-subscriber; the forward fork is carried from the wave-3 two-flavor ratio (COMM-100, COMM-101). The 1.5x-conservative note is the framework's own read against the disclosed segment margin. |
| `COMM-174` | DIRECT-TO-CELL is the model's LEAD market (non-substitutable product, where the capital is flowing, carries the cannibalization optionality), cannibalizing home broadband at the EDGE not the CORE, bounded by beam-saturation physics; near-term D2C served revenue ex-China ~$12-14B by 2030-31 against a ~5.5B-device / ~$1.1T ceiling. Lead by OPTIONALITY and non-substitutability, NOT by current revenue (it is ~10x smaller than fixed broadband on near-term served dollars). | `derived_estimate` | The direct-to-cell-as-lead reframe (NEW framework contribution) | [synthesis/comms_framework_synthesis.md](synthesis/comms_framework_synthesis.md) Sections 1-2; COMM-152 (served revenue / reach), COMM-153 (cannibalization boundary), COMM-035/COMM-036 (ceiling) | Internal claims 5, 6. The reframe is the framework's own; the underlying numbers are carried (COMM-152, COMM-153, COMM-037). The "lead" qualifier (optionality, not biggest current market) must travel with the claim so a cold reader does not read "lead" as "biggest current revenue." |
| `COMM-175` | The SPECTRUM input is a carrier-partnership (FCC SCS lease), not a band purchase: a satellite rides a carrier's licensed band as a gap-filler with instant handset compatibility and near-zero spectrum capex; buying spectrum outright (SpaceX ~$17B EchoStar) is the hyperscale exception. More MHz is the capacity lever (Starlink per-beam scales near-linearly with bandwidth, ~3.1 Mbps on 2x5 MHz to ~18.6 Mbps at full holdings; AST ~120 Mbps on ~40 MHz), which is why ~$17B and ~45 MHz changed hands. | `certified` | The spectrum-as-lease-plus-more-MHz-lever (framework synthesis of COMM-110/COMM-112/COMM-145) | [synthesis/comms_framework_synthesis.md](synthesis/comms_framework_synthesis.md) Sections 2-4; COMM-110 (SCS framework), COMM-111/COMM-112 (the live arrangements and the buy), COMM-145 (per-beam scaling), COMM-150 (AST ~45 MHz) | Internal claims 7, 8. A framework-level restatement of the spectrum-access finding; the underlying facts are carried. The forward spectrum opening is FR3 / 7-15 GHz at WRC-27 (COMM-107), the one greenfield not yet filed, but its orbital reachability for D2C is unresolved. |
| `COMM-176` | The CATALYST is the forced 6G upgrade users will not pay a premium for: ~two-thirds balk above 5 euros/mo even for 10x speed, 5G delivered no ARPU premium (ARPU falling ~1.3-2%/yr), yet 6G capex is forced by traffic/parity physics, so the incumbent's per-subscriber economics DETERIORATE on the next cycle, compressing the incumbent on the exact axis the forward comparison measures. The ground next-upgrade cost X is a new RADIO refresh on existing sites (~$20-50k/site), not new sites; the 6G cycle (~2030-2035) repeats this shape. | `derived_estimate` | The forced-6G catalyst (NEW framework contribution, hypothesis-framed) | [synthesis/comms_framework_synthesis.md](synthesis/comms_framework_synthesis.md) Sections 4-5; COMM-128/COMM-130/COMM-131 (the 6G demand and forced-cost), COMM-117/COMM-122 (X = radio refresh), COMM-025 (~$20-50k/site) | Internal claims 10, 11. The demand finding is carried (COMM-128, COMM-130); the forced-cost framing is a hypothesis (the framework and thesis Revision 4 render no verdict); the X = radio-refresh shape is carried from the 4G-to-5G doc (COMM-117, COMM-122) and the wave-1 site-cost row (COMM-025). |
| `COMM-177` | The Neutron fit and the laser side track inside the framework: Neutron carries ~5 V3-class per launch (~$10-11M/sat, mass-bound) and ~1 D2C BlueBird-Block-2-class per launch (~$50-55M/sat, antenna-stow-bound), the >20x-per-launch V3 economics being Starship-bound; and laser DC-interconnect is a SEPARATE side track (terrestrial = tens-of-Gbps supplement, orbital DC-to-DC = the primary architecture), per-link not per-subscriber, with a different buyer. | `derived_estimate` | The Neutron-fit and side-track placement (framework synthesis) | [synthesis/comms_framework_synthesis.md](synthesis/comms_framework_synthesis.md) Sections 3.3 and the Neutron section; COMM-155/COMM-160 (the Neutron fit), COMM-169/COMM-170 (the laser side track), COMM-133/COMM-140 (Starship-bound V3) | Internal claims 12, 13. Both are carried from the source docs (COMM-155, COMM-160, COMM-169, COMM-170); the framework's contribution is placing them in the model (Neutron's comms role is stow-optimized/dedicated insertion, and the laser side track sits out of the RF consumer spine). The framework is a STRUCTURE, not a populated model; the entrant-specific cost per subscriber remains the open gate. |

| `COMM-227` | Founder-hypothesis verdict: "adding satellites buys COVERAGE up to a floor, then CAPACITY beyond it" is CORRECT as a conceptual ordering; sharpening: real systems overshoot the floor for capacity and the floor is altitude-inflated, so flown counts are capacity-dominated. | `derived_estimate` | The hypothesis verdict (load-bearing) | [direct_communication/leo_constellation_coverage_minimums.md](direct_communication/leo_constellation_coverage_minimums.md) claim 19 | Verdict, not a measured fact; the ordering is conceptually right, the coupling is the nuance. |
| `COMM-228` | A continuity-only US+Europe service is a few-hundred-satellite problem (~130-450 SOC, plausibly lower optimized), distinct from the ~1,584-30,000-satellite capacity build; the floor-to-capacity gap is set by demand density and spectrum, connecting to the density-aware unit (COMM-172). | `derived_estimate` | The thesis implication (load-bearing) | [direct_communication/leo_constellation_coverage_minimums.md](direct_communication/leo_constellation_coverage_minimums.md) claim 20; COMM-172, COMM-220 | Coverage is cheap (few hundred sats); capacity over dense ground is what is expensive. |

## COMM - Communications Claims (Wave 6: the DTC antenna/aperture physics, the Flatellite and V3 platforms, the governing DTC system model, the per-phone operating point, and the coverage geography)

This continues the master reconciliation for communications wave 6 (ingested
2026-06-23), the direct-to-cell antenna-and-architecture wave: six source docs (the
Rocket Lab Flatellite platform, the Starlink V3 platform / why-Starship size story,
the DTC antenna-aperture tradeoff, the single-governing DTC system model, the
per-phone rate and latency operating point, and the DTC coverage geography). Each doc
minted its own in-doc `COMM-###` ledger, so the in-doc IDs and these global IDs are
identical. Allocation: Flatellite `COMM-249`..`COMM-270`; V3 platform
`COMM-271`..`COMM-292`; antenna-aperture tradeoff `COMM-293`..`COMM-314`; system model
`COMM-315`..`COMM-335` + `COMM-356`..`COMM-365`; per-phone rate/latency
`COMM-336`..`COMM-355` + `COMM-371`..`COMM-378`; coverage geography
`COMM-386`..`COMM-405`. `COMM-366`..`COMM-370` and `COMM-379`..`COMM-385` are reserved
and unused. The system-model block (`COMM-315`..`COMM-335` + `COMM-356`..`COMM-365`) is
the WIP GOVERNING MODEL: recorded here as the architecture source of truth, but its doc
([direct_communication/dtc_system_model.md](direct_communication/dtc_system_model.md))
was still being revised at this reconciliation and was not committed with the wave.

### DTC antenna-aperture tradeoff (COMM-293..314)

| Claim ID | Claim text | Source status | Role | Links or internal references | Uncertainty notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `COMM-293` | A standard smartphone for DTC is fixed at ~0.2 W (23 dBm) into a ~0 dBi near-omnidirectional antenna (EIRP ~23 dBm); SAR limits + lack of space for a high-gain element mean none of this can be improved. The phone cannot help close the link. | `certified` | The fixed-and-weak phone side (the whole problem) | [direct_communication/dtc_antenna_aperture_tradeoff.md](direct_communication/dtc_antenna_aperture_tradeoff.md) Section 1.1; Panariello, Frank Rayal, TechTimes | FACT. |
| `COMM-294` | In the DTC uplink at ~1900 MHz to LEO, free-space path loss is ~153 dB, the phone's signal arrives at the satellite at ~-130 dBm, and an LTE QPSK signal needs ~-105 dBm to decode: a ~25 dB deficit the satellite alone must recover. | `sourced_estimate` | The worked handset link-budget deficit | [direct_communication/dtc_antenna_aperture_tradeoff.md](direct_communication/dtc_antenna_aperture_tradeoff.md) Section 1.2; Frank Rayal worked link budget | FACT, single-source on the exact dB numbers (corroborated in shape by AST + Panariello). |
| `COMM-295` | Both link directions improve with one lever: satellite antenna gain (aperture); uplink G/T and downlink EIRP both rise with antenna area, and at parity of bandwidth/frequency/orbit they are the only link-budget parameters. | `certified` | The aperture-is-the-lever rule | [direct_communication/dtc_antenna_aperture_tradeoff.md](direct_communication/dtc_antenna_aperture_tradeoff.md) Section 1.2; Panariello, Qorvo link-budget review | FACT. |
| `COMM-296` | Antenna gain follows G = 4 pi eta A / lambda^2 (effective aperture A, efficiency eta); this is stated in SpaceX/AST direct-to-device FCC technical narratives. | `certified` | The gain-from-aperture formula | [direct_communication/dtc_antenna_aperture_tradeoff.md](direct_communication/dtc_antenna_aperture_tradeoff.md) Section 1; SpaceX Gen2 D2C FCC narrative, arXiv 2506.18672 | FACT, formula. |
| `COMM-297` | Even a thin ~5 MHz / ~32 kbps (messaging-grade) DTC channel requires ~29 dBi of satellite antenna gain, an effective aperture of ~1.4 m^2, about 14x the ~0.1 m^2 / ~17 dBi of a terrestrial base station; this is the aperture floor. | `sourced_estimate` | The aperture floor (~1.4 m^2) | [direct_communication/dtc_antenna_aperture_tradeoff.md](direct_communication/dtc_antenna_aperture_tradeoff.md) Section 1.3; Frank Rayal | FACT, single-source. |
| `COMM-298` | The revealed aperture-to-service ladder: ~1-1.5 m^2 (Lynk) = SMS; small arrays (Globalstar) = messaging; ~25 m^2 (Starlink Gen2) = SMS then a few Mbps/beam; ~64 m^2 (AST Block 1) = broadband; ~223 m^2 (AST Block 2) = up to 120 Mbps/cell. Aperture is the DTC service dial. | `sourced_estimate` | The aperture-to-service ladder (load-bearing) | [direct_communication/dtc_antenna_aperture_tradeoff.md](direct_communication/dtc_antenna_aperture_tradeoff.md) Section 2.1; Panariello, Aerospace America, AST official, arXiv 2506.00283 | FACT for the points; DERIVED for the "dial" reading. |
| `COMM-299` | The aperture-to-rate sensitivity is steep; the literature's "1 dB of link gain improves the rate by ~1.58" is actually the +2 dB factor (corrected in COMM-356: +1 dB = x1.26); the Lynk-to-AST aperture span (~1.4 to ~223 m^2, ~22 dB) is the SMS-to-120-Mbps difference. | `derived_estimate` | The dB-to-rate sensitivity (with the correction flag) | [direct_communication/dtc_antenna_aperture_tradeoff.md](direct_communication/dtc_antenna_aperture_tradeoff.md) Section 2.2; arXiv 2506.18672; corrected by COMM-356/349 | The "1 dB = 1.58x" label is wrong (it is +2 dB); see COMM-356. |
| `COMM-300` | AST goes giant because broadband-to-phone is the top of the aperture ladder; AST states it: "the satellite must provide the necessary signal strength," using 693 sq ft (~64 m^2) Block 1 arrays, "the largest commercial arrays ever deployed in low Earth orbit." | `certified` | AST's own giant-aperture rationale | [direct_communication/dtc_antenna_aperture_tradeoff.md](direct_communication/dtc_antenna_aperture_tradeoff.md) Section 3.1; AST How-It-Works | FACT, AST official. |
| `COMM-301` | AST Block 2's ~223 m^2 array yields a modeled gain of ~42 dBi at 880 MHz (eta ~0.7); AST characterizes its aperture as "north of 40 dBi" vs ~16 dBi for a tower; a 40 MHz beam carries ~120 Mbps. | `derived_estimate` | The Block 2 gain / 120 Mbps-per-cell | [direct_communication/dtc_antenna_aperture_tradeoff.md](direct_communication/dtc_antenna_aperture_tradeoff.md) Section 3.2; arXiv 2506.18672, AST Next-Gen BlueBird | 42 dBi DERIVED in the literature; "north of 40 dBi" single-source; 120 Mbps/cell AST official. |
| `COMM-302` | AST's BlueWalker 3 (the 64 m^2 test article) demonstrated ~21 Mbps to a phone, early validation that a ~64 m^2-class aperture reaches real broadband-ish rates. | `certified` | The BW3 single-device empirical anchor | [direct_communication/dtc_antenna_aperture_tradeoff.md](direct_communication/dtc_antenna_aperture_tradeoff.md) Section 3.2; Aerospace America, AST/trade press; sharpened by COMM-338 | FACT; sharpened to single-device by COMM-338 (device count + test bandwidth UNKNOWN). |
| `COMM-303` | Starlink's DTC payload rides a V2-mini bus (~4.1 x 2.7 m) carrying a ~25 m^2 phased array, an order of magnitude smaller in area than AST Block 2, capping per-satellite DTC capacity but staying flat-stackable. | `certified` | The Starlink moderate-aperture payload | [direct_communication/dtc_antenna_aperture_tradeoff.md](direct_communication/dtc_antenna_aperture_tradeoff.md) Section 4.1; Spaceflight Now (bus), KeepTrack/trade press (~25 m^2) | FACT; the precise 25 m^2 is soft. |

| `COMM-305` | Starlink compensates for its smaller aperture with constellation density (DTC added to a portion of new builds, ~20+ per Falcon 9), so coverage and capacity scale with the thousands-strong fleet; a separate up-to-15,000-sat dedicated D2C fleet is filed. | `derived_estimate` | The many-satellites compensation | [direct_communication/dtc_antenna_aperture_tradeoff.md](direct_communication/dtc_antenna_aperture_tradeoff.md) Section 4.2; Aerospace America, New Space Tracker; cross-ref COMM-277 | FACT/DERIVED. |
| `COMM-306` | Independent estimates put each AST Block 2 array at ~35-40x the area of a Starlink DTC antenna and ~100x the per-satellite DTC capacity; the two operators reach comparable aggregate capacity from opposite ends of the aperture-vs-count tradeoff. | `derived_estimate` | The AST-vs-Starlink capacity ratio | [direct_communication/dtc_antenna_aperture_tradeoff.md](direct_communication/dtc_antenna_aperture_tradeoff.md) Section 4.3; New Space Tracker, TechTimes, Aerospace America | FACT for ~35-40x / ~100x (independent estimate); DERIVED for the comparable-aggregate netting. |
| `COMM-307` | For a given launcher, the moderate-aperture-many-satellites architecture has far lower launch cost per satellite (one launch over ~20+ vs ~1-3 giant) and plausibly lower launch cost per bit at the messaging/low-rate level; the giant-aperture architecture wins per-bit only if broadband-to-phone is what the market pays for. | `derived_estimate` | The per-bit launch-cost tradeoff | [direct_communication/dtc_antenna_aperture_tradeoff.md](direct_communication/dtc_antenna_aperture_tradeoff.md) Section 5; cross-ref neutron_comms_payload_fit.md | DERIVED. |
| `COMM-308` | There is a hard floor on satellite antenna size for DTC: below ~1-1.5 m^2 only intermittent SMS; ~25 m^2 for voice/few-Mbps; ~50-60+ m^2 for broadband-to-phone; below ~1 m^2 the bare-handset link cannot close at a useful service level. | `derived_estimate` | The DTC aperture floor ladder | [direct_communication/dtc_antenna_aperture_tradeoff.md](direct_communication/dtc_antenna_aperture_tradeoff.md) Section 5.2; Frank Rayal (1.4 m^2 floor), Panariello / Aerospace America | DERIVED. |
| `COMM-309` | DTC CAN be flat-stacked many-per-launch only at the messaging/thin-data level; Starlink already flies ~25 m^2-class D2C payloads flat-stacked ~20+ per Falcon 9. A messaging-grade D2C satellite is plausibly multi-per-Neutron; the specific 6-9 count is UNKNOWN. | `derived_estimate` | The low-rung flat-stack regime | [direct_communication/dtc_antenna_aperture_tradeoff.md](direct_communication/dtc_antenna_aperture_tradeoff.md) Section 6.1; Starlink flat-stack precedent; cross-ref neutron_comms_payload_fit.md | DERIVED; specific count UNKNOWN. |
| `COMM-310` | Broadband-to-phone CANNOT be flat-stacked at 6-9 per Neutron: it forces the ~64-223 m^2 giant aperture, which folds into a bulky accordion that goes ~1 per Neutron; RF-aperture packing caps at ~34-48% (phone-booth class). | `derived_estimate` | The high-rung ~1-per-Neutron limit | [direct_communication/dtc_antenna_aperture_tradeoff.md](direct_communication/dtc_antenna_aperture_tradeoff.md) Section 6.2; cross-ref neutron_comms_payload_fit.md, COMM-207 | DERIVED. |
| `COMM-311` | The broadband-vs-DTC asymmetry (the core reason their satellites differ in size): a broadband customer's high-gain dish (Starlink ~1,280-element panel, customer-paid) supplies the ground-side gain so the satellite's user antenna can be small/flat-pack; a DTC customer's bare phone (~0 dBi) supplies nothing, so all that gain lives on the satellite. The big antenna is on the ground for broadband and in orbit for DTC. | `derived_estimate` | The gain-placement asymmetry (load-bearing insight) | [direct_communication/dtc_antenna_aperture_tradeoff.md](direct_communication/dtc_antenna_aperture_tradeoff.md) Section 7; KeepTrack/trade press; cross-ref rf_limited_service.md | DERIVED, multi-source-supported. |
| `COMM-312` | This gain-placement asymmetry is the upstream CAUSE of the corpus's launch asymmetry: a broadband satellite is a flat ~7 m panel at ~5/Neutron (mass-bound) while a broadband-to-phone satellite is a ~223 m^2 folded aperture at ~1/Neutron (size-bound), purely because the gain must live somewhere and a bare phone forces it onto the satellite. | `derived_estimate` | The cause-of-the-mass-vs-size asymmetry | [direct_communication/dtc_antenna_aperture_tradeoff.md](direct_communication/dtc_antenna_aperture_tradeoff.md) Section 7; cross-ref neutron_comms_payload_fit.md, COMM-208 | DERIVED. |
| `COMM-313` | Corollary spectrum asymmetry: broadband rides wide Ku/Ka (dish-closed link runs high-order modulation) while DTC is stuck in thin ~5-10 MHz low-band slices partly because the bare-handset link runs at low spectral efficiency (~0.5-0.6 bps/Hz measured for Starlink SMS); DTC capacity is aperture-and-spectrum-starved on both ends. | `derived_estimate` | The spectrum-starvation corollary | [direct_communication/dtc_antenna_aperture_tradeoff.md](direct_communication/dtc_antenna_aperture_tradeoff.md) Section 7; cross-ref COMM-185/186/187, arXiv 2506.00283 | DERIVED, ties to existing claims. |
| `COMM-314` | Strategic fork for a Neutron-launched DTC business: the aperture ladder is also a launch-economics ladder; Neutron's 5.5 m fairing is comfortable on the low rungs (messaging/thin-data, many-per-launch) and binding on the high rung (broadband-to-phone, ~1 per launch where a Starship-class batch lifter out-scales it). The service ambition, not the launcher, sets the aperture. | `derived_estimate` | The aperture-as-launch-economics fork | [direct_communication/dtc_antenna_aperture_tradeoff.md](direct_communication/dtc_antenna_aperture_tradeoff.md) Section 8 | DERIVED. |

### DTC system model (the GOVERNING MODEL: COMM-315..335 + COMM-356..365)

The governing-model doc ([direct_communication/dtc_system_model.md](direct_communication/dtc_system_model.md)) fixes the link-budget relationships and the two-tier split once; it is the architecture source of truth. It was reserved here as a WIP placeholder during the wave-6 reconciliation and has since LANDED (committed in `research/`, catalogued 2026-06-26); the two collapsed range-rows are now expanded to the full per-claim ledger below. The doc self-labels GOVERNING/source-of-truth (not "WIP"), names ONE remaining open number (per-satellite total capacity, COMM-364), uses [SYNTHESIS] labelling throughout (it does not re-derive, it grounds in the wave-6 source layers), and asks to be reviewed before relied upon. COMM-366..370 are reserved-unused.

| Claim ID | Claim text | Source status | Role | Links or internal references | Uncertainty notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `COMM-315` | The data rate to an unmodified phone is set by four satellite-side levers with the phone held fixed and weak: satellite antenna aperture (gain G = 4 pi eta A / lambda^2), satellite power (EIRP), path loss (a function of altitude; FSPL grows with slant distance squared), and owned spectrum bandwidth (Shannon rate ~ B x log2(1+SNR)). The phone (~0.2 W, ~0 dBi, SAR-capped) cannot be a lever. | `derived_estimate` | The four-levers framing | [direct_communication/dtc_system_model.md](direct_communication/dtc_system_model.md); grounds in COMM-293, COMM-294, COMM-295/296, COMM-229/236, Shannon | SYNTHESIS. |
| `COMM-316` | Path loss (altitude) is a real but WEAK lever: FSPL grows with 20 log10(d) and slant range falls with altitude (~1,125 km at 550 km/25 deg to ~750 km at 350 km/25 deg), so the whole 550-to-350 km span buys back only ~3.5-4 dB; the ~25-to-223 m^2 aperture span is ~9.5 dB and the ~1-to-223 m^2 span ~22 dB, so altitude cannot move a satellite between aperture rungs, only trim a rate within a tier. | `derived_estimate` | Altitude is the weak lever | [direct_communication/dtc_system_model.md](direct_communication/dtc_system_model.md); grounds in COMM-209, COMM-217, COMM-294, COMM-299 | SYNTHESIS. CORRECTED by COMM-356: this entry originally read "1 dB ~ 1.58x rate," which is the +2 dB factor; +1 dB = ~1.26x. Qualitative use, no conclusion changes. |
| `COMM-317` | Satellite COUNT does not set per-phone rate; it sets coverage continuity (below the floor) and total system capacity (above the floor), governed by geometry and Shannon-x-beams respectively. A thousand texting satellites still only text each phone; multi-fold coverage adds handover/diversity and marginal combining gain, a second-order benefit, not a per-phone-rate lever. | `derived_estimate` | Count is not a per-phone-rate lever | [direct_communication/dtc_system_model.md](direct_communication/dtc_system_model.md); grounds in COMM-224, COMM-225, COMM-227 | SYNTHESIS. |
| `COMM-318` | The prior conversational flip ("smaller antenna works if you fly lower" then "broadband-to-phone needs 64-223 m^2 full stop") arose because the aperture ladder (COMM-298) is stated WITHOUT an altitude column and with a single service-class column that blurs two tiers (AST higher altitude, planned ~725-740 km; Starlink ~25 m^2 at ~326-335 km; AST Tier-2 broadband; V3 Tier-1 data). The "broadband needs 64-223 m^2" half is CORRECT for Tier-2; the error was applying it to the V3 ~25 m^2 fleet, which targets Tier-1. Separating the two tiers (not an altitude trade) resolves it. | `derived_estimate` | Diagnosis of the flip | [direct_communication/dtc_system_model.md](direct_communication/dtc_system_model.md); grounds in COMM-298, COMM-277, COMM-300 | SYNTHESIS. CORRECTED by COMM-357 (AST altitude). |
| `COMM-319` | Aperture (plus owned spectrum) sets the service tier; altitude is a weak trim. The altitude delta (~6.7-7.1 dB overhead per COMM-357) is still smaller than the aperture delta (~9.5 dB), so the aperture rung separates them, and the larger gap STRENGTHENS that AST sits at the harder corner. Starlink's own fleet is the existence proof that a moderate aperture reaches Tier-1 on aperture + owned spectrum (~65 MHz EchoStar) + scale, not altitude, and does not reach Tier-2 at any buildable altitude. | `derived_estimate` | Aperture sets the tier, altitude trims | [direct_communication/dtc_system_model.md](direct_communication/dtc_system_model.md); grounds in COMM-300/301, COMM-277/278/303, COMM-280, COMM-357 | SYNTHESIS. CORRECTED by COMM-357 from the ~507-523 km demo altitude. |
| `COMM-320` | THE GOVERNING RULE. APERTURE plus OWNED SPECTRUM set which service tier a DTC satellite reaches; ALTITUDE is a weak (~3.5-4 dB across 550-350 km) trim, and SATELLITE COUNT sets coverage and total capacity, never per-phone rate. A moderate ~25 m^2 (V3-class) aperture reaches Tier-1 4G-grade DTC data (~2-10 Mbps) on aperture + owned spectrum; true Tier-2 broadband-to-phone (~100+ Mbps) requires ~60-223 m^2 (AST-class) and no buildable altitude substitutes; below the ~25 m^2 floor (toward ~1 m^2) you only text no matter how low you fly. "A smaller antenna works if you fly lower" is TRUE only as "a moderate ~25 m^2 aperture reaches Tier 1, mainly on aperture + spectrum," and FALSE below the floor and as a route to Tier-2. | `derived_estimate` | The governing rule (load-bearing) | [direct_communication/dtc_system_model.md](direct_communication/dtc_system_model.md); grounds in COMM-298, COMM-299, COMM-308, COMM-316, COMM-317 | SYNTHESIS, governing rule. |

Correct: +1 dB = ~1.26x (10^0.1 = 1.259), +2 dB = ~1.585x. The multiplicative-rate rule holds only in the low-SNR / power-limited regime, which DTC handset links approximate. The ~42 dBi gain is aperture-and-frequency only, altitude-independent, so the aperture analysis is wholly unaffected; the only change is that the AST-vs-Starlink altitude gap WIDENS from ~180 km / under ~4 dB to ~400 km / ~6.7-7.1 dB overhead. This is the distinction the operating point turns on. | `derived_estimate` | Per-cell not per-phone (carried) | [direct_communication/dtc_system_model.md](direct_communication/dtc_system_model.md); grounds in COMM-336, COMM-337 | SYNTHESIS, carried from COMM-336/337. |

The single-phone PEAK is what the operating point sizes against; the single-phone AVERAGE under load is lower. This confirms COMM-317 with the scheduler mechanism. | `derived_estimate` | The converge/diverge scheduler rule | [direct_communication/dtc_system_model.md](direct_communication/dtc_system_model.md); grounds in COMM-338, COMM-339, COMM-340, COMM-342, COMM-317 | SYNTHESIS, carried. |

The two BW3 quantities are UNKNOWN. |

A ~50 m^2 aperture at low orbit (~350-550 km) on ~20-40 MHz of OWNED spectrum delivers ~25-50 Mbps to a SINGLE lightly-loaded phone (the peak / sole-occupant rate, not the per-cell 120 Mbps aggregate nor the busy-cell ~1 Mbps average). ~64-100 m^2 is NOT needed for the single-phone RATE; the extra aperture above ~50-64 m^2 buys per-cell CAPACITY. This sits at the Tier-1 rung of the governing rule, above the ~25 m^2 floor and below the ~60+ m^2 Tier-2 rung; it instantiates the rule, it does not amend it. | `derived_estimate` | The Tier-1 operating point (load-bearing) | [direct_communication/dtc_system_model.md](direct_communication/dtc_system_model.md); grounds in COMM-347, COMM-355, COMM-320, COMM-344, COMM-360 | SYNTHESIS, the operating point. |
| `COMM-362` | THE APERTURE / SATS-PER-LAUNCH CLIFF. The ~50 m^2 operating-point aperture exceeds Neutron's ~5.5 m fairing as a flat panel (~7.07 m side), so it FOLDS and VOLUME binds, dropping the count to ~3 satellites/Neutron, halved from the ~6 a flat ~25 m^2 panel flies. The flat-to-fold transition is the cliff: ~25 m^2 (flat, fits) ~6/Neutron, ~50 m^2 (folds) ~3, ~64 m^2 ~2, ~223 m^2 ~1; VOLUME binds at every aperture. The folded volume is the one unpublished swing input (COMM-206); the calc is validated on the known Falcon 9 / Starlink counts. | `derived_estimate` | The sats-per-launch cliff | [direct_communication/dtc_system_model.md](direct_communication/dtc_system_model.md); grounds in COMM-206, COMM-310, COMM-204, Neutron envelope | SYNTHESIS. The folded volume (COMM-206) is UNPUBLISHED. |

After the AST altitude correction (COMM-357, planned ~725-740 km), AST does NOT fly low, so this latency edge belongs to the LOW-flying entrant. No MEASURED end-to-end DTC latency is published (a named gap, COMM-353); the propagation floor is solid physics. | `derived_estimate` | Low-orbit latency as a structural edge | [direct_communication/dtc_system_model.md](direct_communication/dtc_system_model.md); grounds in COMM-350, COMM-351, COMM-352, COMM-353, COMM-357 | SYNTHESIS, carried. Every operator needing both serves both with two shells. | `derived_estimate` | The add-a-shell, not-raise-inclination rule | [direct_communication/dtc_coverage_geography.md](direct_communication/dtc_coverage_geography.md) Section 3.3; Wikipedia Starlink, Spaceflight Now, eoPortal Telesat | DERIVED. |
